It's really just that constant feeling of needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong need to perform certain actions or behaviors repeatedly, like checking things, counting, or arranging items in a specific way, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. It's just that constant restlessness I mentioned earlier, but definitely no racing thoughts or anything extreme.\n\nDoctor: Is the restlessness present even when you’re not in situations that require you to sit still, like when you’re lying down in bed trying to fall asleep or just standing quietly?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed it then. When I'm lying in bed trying to go to sleep, I can stay still just fine. It's really only an issue when I'm in a situation where I'm supposed to be sitting down for a while, like at my desk or in a long meeting. I sleep a normal amount, and I don't get periods where I suddenly feel unusually energized or don't need to sleep. My energy is pretty consistent, really. It's just that constant restlessness I mentioned earlier, but definitely no racing thoughts or anything extreme.\n\nDoctor: Is the restlessness present even when you’re not in situations that require you to sit still, like when you’re lying down in bed trying to fall asleep or just standing quietly?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed it then. When I'm lying in bed trying to go to sleep, I can stay still just fine. It's really only an issue when I'm in a situation where I'm supposed to be sitting down for a while, like at my desk or in a long meeting. If I'm just standing somewhere or winding down for the night, I don't get that same restless feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications recently, or are you currently taking any medications that might be causing this restlessness as a side effect?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't started anything new recently. Um, I'm actually not taking any medications at all right now, so it definitely wouldn't be a side effect from that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever taken any medications in the past, like antidepressants or antipsychotics, that might have caused this kind of restlessness as a side effect?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I've never really taken any antidepressants or antipsychotics in the past, so it wouldn't be a side effect from anything like that either.\n\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling ever seem to come up in response to specific thoughts or situations, or does it seem to happen on its own without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't noticed it being tied to any specific thoughts or worries, if that's what you mean. It just sort of... happens on its own. It's really just about the physical situation—like being stuck sitting at my desk or in a long meeting—rather than anything going through my head.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness, does it come with an urge to move your legs, and does moving them make you feel better?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not like an urge to move my legs specifically. It's more of an overall feeling, just like I need to get up and do something. Moving around helps, sure, but it's not just about my legs.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or have trouble staying still when you're in other situations that require you to remain seated or calm, like watching a movie, reading, or talking with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If a meeting runs long at work, or... say we’re having one of those tense family dinners with my in-laws, I just have a hard time staying put. I keep finding reasons to get up. I'll get up to grab some water, or check the oven, or go look at some product samples... really just anything instead of staying in my chair.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt the need to do things a certain number of times, or in a specific way, even if you didn’t want to, to make yourself feel better or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I've never really experienced anything like that. My mood is fine, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energized, needed less sleep, or felt like your thoughts were racing, even if it felt good at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had anything like that. I sleep a normal amount, and I don't get periods where I suddenly feel unusually energized or don't need to sleep. My energy is pretty consistent, really. It's just that constant restlessness I mentioned earlier, but definitely no racing thoughts or anything extreme.\n\nDoctor: Is the restlessness present even when you’re not in situations that require you to sit still, like when you’re lying down in bed trying to fall asleep or just standing quietly?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed it then. When I'm lying in bed trying to go to sleep, I can stay still just fine. It's really only an issue when I'm in a situation where I'm supposed to be sitting down for a while, like at my desk or in a long meeting. If I'm just standing somewhere or winding down for the night, I don't get that same restless feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications recently, or are you currently taking any medications that might be causing this restlessness as a side effect?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't started anything new recently. Um, I'm actually not taking any medications at all right now, so it definitely wouldn't be a side effect from that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever taken any medications in the past, like antidepressants or antipsychotics, that might have caused this kind of restlessness as a side effect?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I've never really taken any antidepressants or antipsychotics in the past, so it wouldn't be a side effect from anything like that either.\n\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling ever seem to come up in response to specific thoughts or situations, or does it seem to happen on its own without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't noticed it being tied to any specific thoughts or worries, if that's what you mean. It just sort of... happens on its own. It's really just about the physical situation—like being stuck sitting at my desk or in a long meeting—rather than anything going through my head.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness, does it come with an urge to move your legs, and does moving them make you feel better?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not like an urge to move my legs specifically. It's more of an overall feeling, just like I need to get up and do something. Moving around helps, sure, but it's not just about my legs.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or have trouble staying still when you're in other situations that require you to remain seated or calm, like watching a movie, reading, or talking with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If a meeting runs long at work, or... say we’re having one of those tense family dinners with my in-laws, I just have a hard time staying put. I keep finding reasons to get up. I'll get up to grab some water, or check the oven, or go look at some product samples... really just anything instead of staying in my chair.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt the need to do things a certain number of times, or in a specific way, even if you didn’t want to, to make yourself feel better or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I've never really experienced anything like that. I don't have to count things or do things in a certain order to feel okay. It's just the restlessness and needing to keep moving, not any sort of routine or ritual I have to follow.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're not feeling worried or fearful, do you sometimes feel an inner tension or discomfort when you're trying to sit still that makes it hard for you to stay calm?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. My husband actually points it out a lot. He says I never really power down.\n\nDoctor: When you feel restless, do you notice yourself fidgeting, tapping your hands or feet, or making other small movements without meaning to?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. I actually do it so much that I have to mute myself on the phone so they don't hear the clicking, or I have to consciously move my hands out of view if we're on a video call. It's more of a physical restlessness, not a worried mind, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where your heart races, you feel short of breath, or you have a strong sense that something bad is going to happen—like a panic attack?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't ever had anything like that. My heart doesn't race, and I don't get short of breath or feel like something terrible is about to happen. I've never had a panic attack. It's really just that constant feeling of needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong need to perform certain actions or behaviors repeatedly, like checking things, counting, or arranging items in a specific way, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I don't have any urges to count or arrange things like that. I mean, with my job as a buyer, I obviously check over my orders and inventory to make sure everything is accurate, but that's just normal double-checking for work. It's definitely not something I feel forced to do repeatedly or against my will.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't felt like that. I mean, I'm generally pretty upbeat. I still enjoy the things I usually do, and I definitely don't feel hopeless or down. My mood is fine, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energized, needed less sleep, or felt like your thoughts were racing, even if it felt good at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had anything like that. I sleep a normal amount, and I don't get periods where I suddenly feel unusually energized or don't need to sleep. My energy is pretty consistent, really. It's just that constant restlessness I mentioned earlier, but definitely no racing thoughts or anything extreme.\n\nDoctor: Is the restlessness present even when you’re not in situations that require you to sit still, like when you’re lying down in bed trying to fall asleep or just standing quietly?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed it then. When I'm lying in bed trying to go to sleep, I can stay still just fine. It's really only an issue when I'm in a situation where I'm supposed to be sitting down for a while, like at my desk or in a long meeting. If I'm just standing somewhere or winding down for the night, I don't get that same restless feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications recently, or are you currently taking any medications that might be causing this restlessness as a side effect?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't started anything new recently. Um, I'm actually not taking any medications at all right now, so it definitely wouldn't be a side effect from that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever taken any medications in the past, like antidepressants or antipsychotics, that might have caused this kind of restlessness as a side effect?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I've never really taken any antidepressants or antipsychotics in the past, so it wouldn't be a side effect from anything like that either.\n\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling ever seem to come up in response to specific thoughts or situations, or does it seem to happen on its own without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't noticed it being tied to any specific thoughts or worries, if that's what you mean. It just sort of... happens on its own. It's really just about the physical situation—like being stuck sitting at my desk or in a long meeting—rather than anything going through my head.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness, does it come with an urge to move your legs, and does moving them make you feel better?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's not like an urge to move my legs specifically. It's more of an overall feeling, just like I need to get up and do something. Moving around helps, sure, but it's not just about my legs.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or have trouble staying still when you're in other situations that require you to remain seated or calm, like watching a movie, reading, or talking with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If a meeting runs long at work, or... say we’re having one of those tense family dinners with my in-laws, I just have a hard time staying put. I keep finding reasons to get up. I'll get up to grab some water, or check the oven, or go look at some product samples... really just anything instead of staying in my chair.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt the need to do things a certain number of times, or in a specific way, even if you didn’t want to, to make yourself feel better or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I've never really experienced anything like that. I don't have to count things or do things in a certain order to feel okay. It's just the restlessness and needing to keep moving, not any sort of routine or ritual I have to follow.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're not feeling worried or fearful, do you sometimes feel an inner tension or discomfort when you're trying to sit still that makes it hard for you to stay calm?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. That's actually a big part of what brings me in. It's this constant restless feeling. It's not worry, it's just... like I'm wound too tight. \n\nI just can't fully settle down. It happens even when I'm doing something where I'm supposed to be calmly sitting, like through a long planning meeting at work, or... or even during my own time when I'm trying to relax, like painting or doing yoga. I just feel that tension and can't fully relax into it.\n\nDoctor: Does the restlessness ever occur when you're not in a situation where you're expected to sit still, like when you're just relaxing at home or sitting quietly by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely does. Even on weekends when I don't have anywhere to be, I don't really just sit quietly. I tend to finish one thing and then immediately start right on another. Like, I'll do a load of laundry, then check some work emails, then start reorganizing the product samples I brought home, or go touch up a painting... I just keep moving from task to task. My husband actually points it out a lot. He says I never really power down.\n\nDoctor: When you feel restless, do you notice yourself fidgeting, tapping your hands or feet, or making other small movements without meaning to?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. When I'm on calls with suppliers for work, my foot is usually bouncing under the desk. And I'm always clicking a pen or twisting my ring around. I actually do it so much that I have to mute myself on the phone so they don't hear the clicking, or I have to consciously move my hands out of view if we're on a video call. It's just constant little movements like that.\n\nDoctor: Is the restlessness something you feel throughout the day, even when you're not in specific situations that require sitting still or when you're not actively doing tasks?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. If I'm not stuck in a chair or trying to settle down for something, I don't really notice it. Like, if I'm just up and about going through my day, I feel fine. It's not a constant, all-day feeling... it really just seems to flare up when I'm expected to be still or when I'm trying to pause.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't feel the need to do things a certain number of times or in a specific order, do you ever feel that you have to perform certain actions in a particular way to relieve the restlessness, even if it's not to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. It's not like I have a specific way I need to do things to feel better. The fidgeting... like the pen clicking or my foot bouncing, it just sort of happens on its own. I don't have to do it in a certain pattern or anything like that. It's really just random movement.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or have trouble staying still even when you're engaged in activities that naturally involve movement or when you're not required to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm already moving around—like if I'm walking through the warehouse or just doing stuff around the house—I don't feel that restlessness. It's really only an issue when I'm forced to stop and sit still. If I'm active, I feel totally fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
